- Idea: MBSR framed attention to bodily sensation as a secular clinical skill; its body-first practices became the default template that many biofeedback and wellbeing technologies imitate, often without the non-striving attitude.
- What it is: An eight-week course in which participants learn the body scan, sitting meditation on the breath and gentle yoga, and bring non-judging attention to pain, stress and daily routines.
- How it works: Weekly group sessions, a day-long retreat and daily home practice with guided recordings; started at the University of Massachusetts Medical School in 1979.
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, Care & healing, First-person & felt experience
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=oqWIqRwCzJA
- Images: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/b/b8/Jon_Kabat-Zinn_%282018%29.png
- Project page: https://www.ummhealth.org/center-mindfulness

#### Mindful Breathing and Walking Meditation (Plum Village) — Thich Nhat Hanh (1975)
- Type: Somatic practice · Body & senses: Breath, Movement & kinesthesia
- Idea: Mindfulness is bodily: coming back to breath and feet reunites body and mind in the present moment.
- What it is: Walking slowly and matching steps to in- and out-breaths ('I have arrived, I am home'), and returning to the breath when a bell sounds; taught in The Miracle of Mindfulness (1975) and at Plum Village.
- How it works: Everyday practices of mindful breathing, walking, eating and bells of mindfulness, spread through books, retreats and the Plum Village community founded in 1982.
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, Body-mind unity
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=90Pzn6NK4VQ
- Images: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/1/1b/Thich_Nhat_Hanh_12_%28cropped%29.jpg
- Project page: https://plumvillage.org/practice/walking-meditation

#### Deep Listening — Pauline Oliveros (1971)
- Type: Somatic practice · Body & senses: Voice, sound & listening, Breath, Whole body-mind
- Idea: Oliveros distinguished hearing from listening: listening is a bodily, relational attention that can be trained, which invites designers to treat sound as something the whole soma takes part in.
- What it is: Groups perform text scores that ask them to listen to everything, including their own body sounds and the room, and to sound or stay silent in response; workshops mix body work, dreams and listening walks.
- How it works: Listening and sounding exercises, text scores (Sonic Meditations, 1971) and group retreats; named Deep Listening after a 1988 recording in the Fort Worden cistern.
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, Intercorporeality, More-than-human
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Qgfgve0quYE
- Images: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/0/0e/OliverosOM20.jpg
- Project page: https://www.deeplistening.rpi.edu

#### Vipassana Meditation (as taught by S. N. Goenka) — S. N. Goenka (1969)
- Type: Somatic practice · Body & senses: Breath, Whole body-mind, Pain & inner sensations
- Idea: The technique treats mind and body as one flow of changing sensation and trains equanimity towards it; it is a direct source of the clinical body scan that soma design workshops use.
- What it is: In a silent ten-day residential course, meditators first watch the breath at the nostrils (Anapana) and then sweep attention systematically through bodily sensations, observing them without reacting.
- How it works: Ten-day silent retreats with fixed timetables, recorded instructions and evening discourses, offered free worldwide since Goenka began teaching in India in 1969.
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, First-person & felt experience, Body-mind unity
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EmcgGP-RSGc
- Images: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/b/bf/Global_Vipassana_Pagoda_2.jpg
- Project page: https://www.dhamma.org

#### The Perceptible Breath (Der Erfahrbare Atem) — Ilse Middendorf (1965)
- Type: Somatic practice · Body & senses: Breath, Voice, sound & listening, Touch & pressure
- Idea: Middendorf's rule 'I let my breath come, I let it go, and wait until it comes by itself' contrasts with breath-pacing technology: the designer's job may be to make breath perceptible rather than to control it.
- What it is: Sitting or lying, people let the breath come and go by itself and sense where it moves in the body, using pressure points, stretches, vowel sounds and a teacher's touch to explore breath 'spaces'.
- How it works: Breath sensing with pressure points, movement, vowel resonance and hands-on work; Middendorf founded her breath-therapy institute in Berlin in 1965.
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, First-person & felt experience, Body-mind unity
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=i7Ys151xqhg
- Project page: https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilse_Middendorf

#### Sensory Awareness — Charlotte Selver, Elsa Gindler (1938)
- Type: Somatic practice · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Breath, Touch & pressure
- Idea: Gindler and Selver held that everyday acts, done attentively, are enough: noticing how you sit is already practice, which licenses designers to work with ordinary acts rather than special exercises.
- What it is: In a class, people simply sit, stand, lie, walk, lift a stone or touch a partner's head, and are asked open questions about what they sense, with no correct form to reach.
- How it works: Experiential 'experiments' in everyday activities guided by questions rather than instructions, from Gindler's Berlin work; Selver taught it in the US from 1938 and at Esalen from 1963.
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, First-person & felt experience, Body-mind unity
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fDSKUBt-Z9k
- Project page: https://www.sensoryawareness.org

### Somatic Dance & Improvisation

Contact Improvisation, Authentic Movement, Continuum, Halprin, Butoh, Deborah Hay.

#### Gaga — Ohad Naharin (2003)
- Type: Somatic practice · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Whole body-mind
- Idea: Gaga puts listening to sensation before telling the body what to do, and keeps earlier instructions active while new ones arrive; it is a model for designing layered, continuous guidance rather than step-by-step commands.
- What it is: A teacher talks continuously, layering images such as 'float', 'shaking' or letting the body melt, while dancers or non-dancers keep moving for an hour without mirrors, attending to sensation, effort and pleasure.
- How it works: Improvised classes guided by layered verbal imagery, without mirrors, in Gaga/dancers and Gaga/people tracks; developed with Batsheva from the 1990s and named Gaga in 2003.
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, Pleasure & play, Movement & expression
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CBTWp-6PgFQ
- Images: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/de/Ohad_Naharin._Herzliya_Conference_2016_2034_%28cropped%29.jpg
- Project page: https://www.gagapeople.com

#### My Body, The Buddhist — Deborah Hay (2000)
- Type: Book & essay · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Movement & kinesthesia
- Idea: Hay gives the body agency in the creative process and treats perception as the dance; for designers it shows how to write from the body in the first person.
- What it is: Nineteen short chapters with titles such as 'my body likes to rest' and 'my body is bored by answers', in which Hay reports what her dancing body taught her about attention and self.
- How it works: Short first-person essays linking choreographic practice and Buddhist ideas of the self, with a foreword by Susan Foster; Wesleyan University Press, 2000.
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Attunement & appreciation, Body-mind unity
- Paper: https://www.weslpress.org/9780819563286/my-body-the-buddhist/ (Wesleyan University Press 2000)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nLbGprgCoOo
- Images: https://wesleyan-university-us.imgix.net/covers/9780819563286.jpg

#### Underscore — Nancy Stark Smith (1990)
- Type: Somatic practice · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Touch & pressure, Whole body-mind
- Idea: The Underscore gives structure to emergence: a common score of states lets many bodies improvise together without a leader, a model for designing open-ended collective somatic experiences.
- What it is: A long group improvisation, often three hours or more, that follows a shared map of phases: arriving alone, the 'small dance', expanding into space and contact with others, and slowly returning to stillness and reflection.
- How it works: Scored group improvisation with named phases and graphic symbols, developed by Nancy Stark Smith from 1990 within the Contact Improvisation community.
- Lens: Intercorporeality, Attunement & appreciation
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BFWRF0WXkAs
- Project page: https://contactquarterly.com

#### Body Weather — Min Tanaka (1985)
- Type: Somatic practice · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Touch & pressure, Whole body-mind
- Idea: The body is weather: always changing and shaped by its environment, so training means becoming sensitive to forces rather than fixing a technique.
- What it is: Dancers train through farm labour, a demanding physical workout and 'manipulations', in which one partner, eyes closed, is moved and touched by another; Min Tanaka founded Body Weather Farm in 1985.
- How it works: Workshops and farm residencies combining 'MB' (muscles/bones) conditioning, partnered manipulations and image-based improvisation.
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, Estrangement, Intercorporeality
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eLKSUUQVIuo
- Images: https://www.bodyweather.net/index_files/dequinceyco-body-weather2.jpg
- Project page: https://www.bodyweather.net

#### Six Viewpoints — Mary Overlie (1978)
- Type: Somatic practice · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Whole body-mind
- Idea: The Viewpoints treat attention as a skill that can be split into channels; designers use this to structure what participants notice in embodied ideation and bodystorming.
- What it is: Performers practise noticing and working with six materials of performance, space, shape, time, emotion, movement and story, one at a time, in solo and group improvisations.
- How it works: Improvisation exercises organised by six 'viewpoints', taught in NYU's Experimental Theater Wing since 1978 and later adapted by Anne Bogart and Tina Landau.
- Lens: Intercorporeality, Movement & expression, Attunement & appreciation
- Video: https://vimeo.com/160501353
- Images: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/da/Mary_Overlie.jpg
- Project page: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Overlie

#### Contact Improvisation — Steve Paxton, Nancy Stark Smith (1972)
- Type: Somatic practice · Body & senses: Touch & pressure, Posture, balance & proprioception, Movement & kinesthesia
- Idea: Contact Improvisation shows that two bodies can think together through touch, faster than conscious decision; for designers of social touch it offers a trained vocabulary of listening through skin and weight.
- What it is: Two or more dancers keep a rolling point of physical contact, sharing weight, falling, lifting and following momentum; the 'Small Dance' of standing still and sensing the reflexes that keep you upright is its basic exercise.
- How it works: Duet improvisation through shared weight, rolling contact and reflexes, practised in classes and open jams; initiated by Steve Paxton in 1972 (Magnesium) and documented in the Videoda film Fall After Newton (1987).
- Lens: Intercorporeality, Movement & expression, Pleasure & play
- Video: https://vimeo.com/181794954
- Images: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/3/3e/Contact_Improvisation_Trio_Dojo_Florence.jpg https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/e/e9/Steve_Paxton_2012.jpg
- Project page: https://contactquarterly.com

#### Skinner Releasing Technique — Joan Skinner (1966)
- Type: Somatic practice · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Posture, balance & proprioception
- Idea: In Skinner Releasing, technique and creativity are the same act: alignment is found through imagination rather than correction, a stance close to soma design's joining of craft and felt experience.
- What it is: Dancers are led through poetic images, such as being suspended by threads or moving like seaweed, often with a partner's hands-on support, until held tension releases and movement becomes spontaneous.
- How it works: Image-guided classes, partner 'hands-on' studies and improvisation, first shared with students at the University of Illinois in 1966–67.
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, Movement & expression
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w_kvkPfI07c
- Project page: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skinner_Releasing_Technique

#### Ankoku Butoh (Dance of Darkness) — Tatsumi Hijikata (1959)
- Type: Somatic practice · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Whole body-mind
- Idea: Dancing from inner images rather than steps lets the body become other than itself, a method for making the habitual body strange.
- What it is: A dance form begun by Hijikata's Kinjiki (1959) in which dancers move very slowly, crouched and distorted, guided by image scores (butoh-fu) that ask them to become insects, ash or a body being eaten.
- How it works: Training through butoh-fu image notations, walking exercises and extreme slowness, developed by Hijikata with Kazuo Ohno in 1960s Tokyo.
- Lens: Estrangement, Movement & expression, Body politics
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hNV0T5zI7VI
- Images: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/1/16/Tatsumi_Hijikata.jpg
- Project page: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butoh

#### Authentic Movement — Mary Starks Whitehouse, Janet Adler (1958)
- Type: Somatic practice · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Whole body-mind
- Idea: The practice separates 'I move' from 'I am moved' and trains a non-judging inner witness, a first-person method that designers can borrow for describing felt experience without evaluation.
- What it is: A mover closes their eyes and waits for movement to arise, following it without planning, while a witness sits at the edge and watches; afterwards both speak about their experience in the first person.
- How it works: Eyes-closed movement with an external witness and first-person spoken reflection; from Whitehouse's Jungian 'movement in depth' (1950s), developed by Janet Adler into the mover–witness discipline.
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Intercorporeality, Body-mind unity
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fcZGUTy5wYk
- Images: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/91/Movers.jpg
- Project page: https://disciplineofauthenticmovement.com

### Somatic Therapy & Healing

Focusing, Somatic Experiencing, Hakomi and body-based approaches to trauma and pain.

#### The Body Keeps the Score — Bessel van der Kolk (2014)
- Type: Book & essay · Body & senses: Pain & inner sensations, Heartbeat, Whole body-mind
- Idea: The book brought the idea that recovery must go through the body to a mass audience; some of its neuroscience claims are contested, so designers should read it as a framing rather than as evidence.
- What it is: Van der Kolk describes how trauma changes brain and body, including interoception and heart rate variability, and reviews treatments from EMDR and neurofeedback to yoga and theatre.
- How it works: Clinical narrative and research review on trauma, interoception and body-based therapies; Viking, 2014.
- Lens: Care & healing, Body-mind unity
- Paper: https://www.penguinrandomhouse.com/books/313183/the-body-keeps-the-score-by-bessel-van-der-kolk-md/ (Viking 2014)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iTefkqYQz8g
- Images: https://images1.penguinrandomhouse.com/smedia/9780143127741

#### Waking the Tiger: Healing Trauma — Peter A. Levine (1997)
- Type: Book & essay · Body & senses: Pain & inner sensations, Whole body-mind
- Idea: The book treats the body as healer and trauma as a physiological process, offering designers a cautious model for working with arousal and safety.
- What it is: Levine asks why wild animals, threatened constantly, rarely stay traumatised, and describes how humans can let frozen survival energy discharge through felt sensation.
- How it works: Popular book in four parts (the body as healer, symptoms, transformation, first aid) with self-exercises; North Atlantic Books, 1997, with Ann Frederick.
- Lens: Care & healing, Body-mind unity
- Paper: https://penguinrandomhousehighereducation.com/book/?isbn=9781556432330 (North Atlantic Books 1997)
- Images: https://images.penguinrandomhouse.com/cover/9781556432330

#### Somatic Experiencing — Peter A. Levine (1989)
- Type: Somatic practice · Body & senses: Pain & inner sensations, Whole body-mind, Movement & kinesthesia
- Idea: Levine treats trauma as an incomplete bodily response rather than only a memory; his ideas of titration (small doses) and pendulation (swinging between states) are useful pacing rules for any design that stirs strong feeling.
- What it is: A practitioner helps a client track small bodily sensations, such as warmth, trembling or the urge to push, and moves attention slowly between activation and resources so that stuck survival responses can complete.
- How it works: Talk-and-sensation sessions using interoceptive tracking, titration and pendulation, developed by Levine from the 1970s and formalised under this name in the late 1980s; clinical evidence is still limited.
- Lens: Care & healing, First-person & felt experience, Body-mind unity
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ByalBx85iC8
- Project page: https://traumahealing.org

#### Hakomi Method — Ron Kurtz (1981)
- Type: Somatic practice · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Touch & pressure
- Idea: Hakomi assumes that core beliefs are organised in the body and can be studied in mindful states; its gentle 'probes' are a template for designing stimuli that reveal rather than direct felt experience.
- What it is: The client settles into mindfulness and the therapist offers a small experiment, such as a phrase ('you are welcome here') or a light touch, then both study the spontaneous bodily and emotional reaction.
- How it works: Mindfulness-based assisted self-study with verbal and touch 'probes', built on principles of nonviolence, organicity and mind-body holism; developed by Ron Kurtz in the 1970s, taught by the Hakomi Institute since 1981.
- Lens: Care & healing, First-person & felt experience, Attunement & appreciation
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rcRda7-tsXU
- Project page: https://hakomiinstitute.com

#### Rosen Method Bodywork — Marion Rosen (1980)
- Type: Somatic practice · Body & senses: Touch & pressure, Breath, Pain & inner sensations
- Idea: Rosen Method's touch listens rather than manipulates; it models how a device that touches the body could invite awareness instead of correcting posture.
- What it is: The client lies on a table while the practitioner rests hands on places where breath does not move and muscles are held, and gently names what they notice, so feelings connected to that holding can surface.
- How it works: Gentle 'listening' touch combined with dialogue about sensation and breath, rooted in Gindler's work via Lucy Heyer; the Rosen Institute was formed in 1980.
- Lens: Technology that touches back, Care & healing, Attunement & appreciation
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HuGMM9njS24
- Project page: https://rosenmethod.org

#### Focusing — Eugene Gendlin (1978)
- Type: Book & essay · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Pain & inner sensations
- Idea: It makes the felt sense practical: a designer can use the same steps to articulate what a prototype feels like before having words for it.
- What it is: Gendlin's popular book teaches the six Focusing steps for finding and staying with a bodily 'felt sense' of a problem until it shifts.
- How it works: A step-by-step manual with examples from therapy and everyday life; Everest House 1978, later Bantam.
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Body-mind unity
- Paper: https://www.penguinrandomhouse.com/books/59035/focusing-by-eugene-tgendlin-phd/ (Everest House 1978)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pFIBvofcRNA
- Images: https://images4.penguinrandomhouse.com/smedia/9780553278330

#### Tamalpa Life/Art Process — Anna Halprin, Daria Halprin (1978)
- Type: Somatic practice · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Whole body-mind
- Idea: The process assumes that body, feeling and imagination speak the same story in different languages, so moving between them (the 'psychokinetic visualisation' loop) deepens both; designers can borrow this loop for somatic reflection.
- What it is: Participants move a theme from their life, draw what they felt, speak or write about it, and dance again, cycling between body, image and word; Anna Halprin developed it from her own dances through cancer.
- How it works: Movement, drawing and verbal reflection in a structured cycle, informed by the RSVP Cycles scoring method; taught by the Tamalpa Institute founded in 1978.
- Lens: Body-mind unity, Care & healing, Movement & expression
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yVQ50oG2bnE
- Images: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/77/Anna_Halprin_Breath_Made_Visible.jpg https://tamalpa.org/wp-content/uploads/about_03-1200x550.jpg
- Project page: https://www.tamalpa.org

#### Rolfing Structural Integration — Ida Rolf (1971)
- Type: Somatic practice · Body & senses: Touch & pressure, Posture, balance & proprioception, Whole body-mind
- Idea: Rolf treated posture as an organisation of the whole person in gravity, with emotional and bodily holding inseparable; its health claims are not supported by evidence, but it shaped how somatics thinks about fascia and touch.
- What it is: A series of ten hands-on sessions in which a practitioner works slowly and deeply on connective tissue and gives movement prompts, aiming to reorganise how the whole body stands and moves in gravity.
- How it works: Ten-session manual therapy protocol ('the recipe') on fascia combined with movement education, developed from the 1940s and taught by the Rolf Institute founded in 1971.
- Lens: Technology that touches back, Body-mind unity
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9_Vwd7-GA3o
- Images: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/7b/Rolfing.jpg
- Project page: https://rolf.org

#### Focusing — Eugene Gendlin (1969)
- Type: Somatic practice · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Pain & inner sensations
- Idea: Gendlin showed that meaning is bodily before it is verbal: the felt sense knows more than we can say, which grounds first-person methods where designers articulate what a design feels like.
- What it is: A person turns attention to the middle of the body and waits for a vague 'felt sense' of a situation, then tries words or images against it until one fits and the body registers a 'felt shift'.
- How it works: Six steps (clearing a space, felt sense, handle, resonating, asking, receiving), practised alone or in listening partnerships; first described in a 1969 paper from Gendlin's psychotherapy research with Carl Rogers.
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Body-mind unity, Attunement & appreciation
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1037/h0088716 (Psychotherapy: Theory, Research & Practice 1969)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4tvGdqIA27I
- Project page: https://focusing.org

#### Dance/Movement Therapy — Marian Chace (1942)
- Type: Somatic practice · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Whole body-mind
- Idea: Chace showed that mirroring another's movement is a form of empathy that can reach where words cannot; designers of synchrony and shared biosignals are working in the same territory.
- What it is: In a Chace circle, a therapist picks up and reflects each participant's movements and rhythms, builds a shared group rhythm and uses it to open expression and contact among people who may not speak.
- How it works: Group circles with mirroring, rhythmic group action, symbolism and verbal reflection; begun by Chace with psychiatric patients at St. Elizabeths Hospital in 1942.
- Lens: Care & healing, Intercorporeality, Movement & expression
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0lEYgtyLA8k
- Project page: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marian_Chace

## Soma Design Methods

How to design from the body: first-person inquiry, estrangement, somatic training of the designer, bodystorming, material exploration, articulating felt experience.

### First-Person & Autobiographical Methods

The designer's own felt experience as data: first-person research, autoethnography, autobiographical design.

#### Validity and Rigour in Soma Design: Sketching with the Soma — Anna Ståhl, Vasiliki Tsaknaki, Madeline Balaam (2021)
- Type: Paper · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Movement & kinesthesia
- Idea: The designer's felt sense can be trained, tested against others and made accountable; that is where the rigour of soma design lies.
- What it is: Describes 'sketching with the soma' — the iterative, felt trying-out of materials and movements on one's own body — and argues for what makes such first-person design rigorous and valid.
- How it works: Analysis of several soma design processes, identifying practices of sensitisation, iteration and shared articulation.
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Body-mind unity
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3470132 (ACM TOCHI 28(6), 2021)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MGYlVdYWcsg

#### The Designer's Body as Resource in Design — Dag Svanæs (2020)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Whole body-mind
- Idea: Imagining, remembering and enacting a felt experience are different bodily resources; a designer can switch among them on purpose.
- What it is: Maps how designers use their own bodies in design by combining point of view (first, second, third person) with tense (past, present, future), and shows each combination in practice.
- How it works: Conceptual framework grounded in Merleau-Ponty and illustrated with student design projects and the authors' practice.
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Body-mind unity
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3313831.3376430 (CHI 2020)

#### A Sample of One: First-Person Research Methods in HCI — Andrés Lucero, Audrey Desjardins, Carman Neustaedter, Kristina Höök (2019)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Whole body-mind
- Idea: When the researcher is also the participant, the body that lives with the design becomes the instrument of knowing.
- What it is: A DIS workshop that gathered autoethnography, autobiographical design, soma design and other first-person methods to discuss their rigour, ethics and value in HCI.
- How it works: Workshop with position papers and hands-on sessions; it led to a TOCHI special issue on first-person methods (2021).
- Lens: First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3301019.3319996 (DIS 2019 Companion)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VXmJNp9R-m4

#### Embracing First-Person Perspectives in Soma-Based Design — Kristina Höök, Katherine Isbister, Caroline Hummels, Thecla Schiphorst (2018)
- Type: Paper · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Movement & kinesthesia
- Idea: To design for felt experience, designers must first examine their own; first-person inquiry is disciplined practice, not self-indulgence.
- What it is: A 22-author paper in which soma designers each describe a first-person design case and together argue that the designer's own felt experience is a legitimate source of design knowledge.
- How it works: Collective paper that grew out of the CHI 2017 Soma-Based Design Theory workshop, gathering cases from breathing, dance, heat, sound and play.
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Body-mind unity
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.3390/informatics5010008 (Informatics 5(1):8, 2018)
- Images: https://pub.mdpi-res.com/informatics/informatics-05-00008/article_deploy/html/images/informatics-05-00008-g001.png
- Project page: https://www.mdpi.com/2227-9709/5/1/8

#### Revealing Tensions in Autobiographical Design in HCI — Audrey Desjardins (2018)
- Type: Paper · Body & senses: Whole body-mind
- Idea: Using your own life as data demands care about what you expose and how honestly you report your own body and home.
- What it is: Reflects on the tensions of autobiographical design — between researcher and participant, private life and publication, genuine need and research agenda — using the authors' own long-term projects.
- How it works: Reflective analysis of the authors' autobiographical design projects (with Aubree Ball), structured as five tensions.
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Body politics
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3196709.3196781 (DIS 2018)

#### Autobiographical Design in HCI Research — Carman Neustaedter, Phoebe Sengers (2012)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Whole body-mind
- Idea: Long, genuine use by the designer's own life and body surfaces the small frictions and pleasures that short studies never reach.
- What it is: Defines autobiographical design — building and living with a system you made for yourself — and shows through interviews with practitioners that it yields knowledge ordinary studies miss.
- How it works: Interview study with researchers who had designed for themselves, synthesised into features of the method.
- Lens: First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/2317956.2318034 (DIS 2012)

#### Transferring Qualities from Horseback Riding to Design — Kristina Höök (2010)
- Type: Paper · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Posture, balance & proprioception, Touch & pressure
- Idea: Years of bodily learning with another living body reveal interaction qualities — subtlety, rhythm, mutual attunement — that screens rarely offer.
- What it is: Höök's autoethnography of learning to ride a horse: how rider and horse come to communicate through subtle shifts of weight and muscle, and which of those qualities design could borrow.
- How it works: First-person autoethnographic account drawn from the author's years of riding lessons, analysed for design qualities.
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Intercorporeality, More-than-human
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/1868914.1868943 (NordiCHI 2010)

#### Closer: Performance, Technologies, Phenomenology — Susan Kozel (2007)
- Type: Book & essay · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Whole body-mind
- Idea: The researcher's dancing body can be a precise instrument for understanding technology, if its experience is described with care.
- What it is: Kozel's book that researches telematic dance, motion capture and wearables from inside her own performing body, developing phenomenology as a hands-on research method.
- How it works: Book (MIT Press, Leonardo series) combining Merleau-Ponty's phenomenology with first-person accounts of the author's performances.
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Intercorporeality, Movement & expression
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.7551/mitpress/9780262113106.001.0001 (MIT Press 2007)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mv7Vp3NPKw4
- Images: https://covers.openlibrary.org/b/isbn/9780262113106-L.jpg?default=false

### Estrangement & Making Strange

Disrupting habitual movement and perception to notice the body anew.

#### Estrangement through Silence — Jonas Fritsch, Kristina Höök, Claudia Núñez-Pacheco, Anna Ståhl (2025)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Voice, sound & listening, Whole body-mind
- Idea: Silence is not an absence but a presence full of sound and contact; shared silence tunes designers to themselves, each other and the environment.
- What it is: Over four workshops across 18 months, mostly outdoors, the authors kept long periods of shared silence; the collective silence worked as an estrangement method that revealed how people and things shape one another.
- How it works: Four first-person workshops with prolonged collective silence during activities such as cooking and designing (with Pedro Sanches and Vasiliki Tsaknaki).
- Lens: Estrangement, Attunement & appreciation, Intercorporeality
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3715336.3735711 (DIS 2025)

#### Embodied Design Ideation Methods: Analysing the Power of Estrangement — Danielle Wilde, Oscar Tomico (2017)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Whole body-mind
- Idea: Disturbing how the body usually moves and senses is a reliable way to make designers notice possibilities they would otherwise overlook.
- What it is: Compares embodied ideation methods from three design practices and shows how estrangement — odd props, unusual constraints, unfamiliar ways of moving — opens up new design ideas.
- How it works: Comparative analysis of workshop methods by Wilde, Vallgårda and Tomico, identifying strategies of estrangement.
- Lens: Estrangement, First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3025453.3025873 (CHI 2017)

#### Moving and Making Strange: An Embodied Approach to Movement-Based Interaction Design — Lian Loke, Toni Robertson (2013)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Posture, balance & proprioception
- Idea: Only when a familiar movement becomes strange do we feel it again; that renewed feeling is the starting point for design.
- What it is: A design methodology with tools for making habitual movement strange, so that designers can notice, describe and design from how movement feels, looks and is sensed by machines.
- How it works: Methodology with three perspectives (mover, observer, machine), using movement scores, somatic exercises and notation, built from dance-based design projects.
- Lens: Estrangement, Movement & expression, First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/2442106.2442113 (ACM TOCHI 20(1), 2013)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7cGcSdDeN9E

#### Making by Making Strange: Defamiliarization and the Design of Domestic Technologies — Genevieve Bell, Phoebe Sengers (2005)
- Type: Paper · Body & senses: Whole body-mind
- Idea: What is most familiar — including our own bodily habits — is hardest to design for until it is made strange.
- What it is: Uses ethnographic and historical material to make the ordinary home look strange, so designers can question assumptions built into domestic technology.
- How it works: Defamiliarization strategies from literary theory and anthropology (cross-cultural comparison, historical reading, cultural critique).
- Lens: Estrangement
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/1067860.1067862 (ACM TOCHI 12(2), 2005)

### Somatic Training of the Designer

Feldenkrais lessons, body scans and movement practice to sharpen the designer's sensitivity (somaesthetic appreciation).

#### Towards a Contemplative Research Framework for Training Self-Observation in HCI — Kristina Mah, Lian Loke (2021)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Breath
- Idea: Observing one's own body-mind is a skill; contemplative traditions offer tested ways to train it.
- What it is: Uses the researcher's own practice of Tonglen compassion meditation to propose a framework for training self-observation, so first-person HCI research can rest on cultivated attention.
- How it works: Autoethnographic study of compassion cultivation combined with micro-phenomenological description, synthesised into a contemplative research framework.
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, First-person & felt experience, Care & healing
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3471932 (ACM TOCHI 28(6), 2021)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xDm2kT4B-kA

#### Teaching Soma Design — Vasiliki Tsaknaki, Madeline Balaam, Anna Ståhl, Kristina Höök (2019)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Movement & kinesthesia, Heat & temperature
- Idea: Somaesthetic sensitivity can be taught; slowing down with the body first changes what students go on to design.
- What it is: Reports soma design courses in which students did Feldenkrais-style body scans and movement exercises, then explored heat, vibration and shape-change on their own bodies before designing.
- How it works: Analysis of several soma design courses and workshops with somatic exercises, material explorations and design briefs.
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3322276.3322327 (DIS 2019)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=taUFMwrBHxE

#### Cultivating Kinaesthetic Awareness through Interaction — Yves Candau, Jules Françoise, Sarah Fdili Alaoui, Thecla Schiphorst (2017)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Voice, sound & listening, Posture, balance & proprioception
- Idea: Awareness of movement grows through attention practices; interaction can support that growth rather than replace it.
- What it is: Draws on somatic practices and embodied cognition to propose how interactive systems — here movement sonification — can help people cultivate kinaesthetic awareness.
- How it works: Theoretical synthesis of Feldenkrais-style somatic education and enactive cognition, grounded in a movement-sound design study.
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, Movement & expression
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3077981.3078042 (MOCO 2017)

#### Move to be Moved — Kristina Höök, Anna Ståhl, Toni Robertson, Thecla Schiphorst (2016)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Whole body-mind
- Idea: You cannot learn to design for movement by talking about it; the designer's own body has to be moved.
- What it is: A CHI 2016 workshop where participants learned soma design by moving: somatic exercises, sharing of movement-based design cases and hands-on exploration.
- How it works: Workshop organised by a large group of movement and soma design researchers, alternating movement practice with discussion.
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, Movement & expression
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/2851581.2856470 (CHI 2016 Extended Abstracts)

#### Practicing Somaesthetics — Wonjun Lee, Richard Shusterman (2014)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Movement & kinesthesia
- Idea: Training the designer's own body awareness before ideation shifts ideas toward bodily experience: somatic practice is a design tool.
- What it is: Design students did somaesthetic awareness exercises before ideating interactive products, and the study compared their ideas with those produced without the practice.
- How it works: Workshop study (likely comparing practice and control conditions) with somaesthetic exercises drawn from Shusterman's pragmatic somaesthetics, followed by ideation and analysis of the resulting concepts.
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/2598510.2598561 (DIS 2014)

#### Somatic Techniques for Experience Design (CHI courses) — Thecla Schiphorst, Lian Loke, Kristina Höök (2013)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Movement & kinesthesia, Breath
- Idea: Designers can learn, in a few hours, to use breath, attention and movement as instruments for sensing experience.
- What it is: Hands-on CHI courses — 'Body, Whys & Videotape' (2013) and 'Designing for Sensory Appreciation' (2020) — that teach HCI practitioners somatic exercises for designing and evaluating experience.
- How it works: Course with case studies, video and in-class somatic exercises drawn from Laban, Feldenkrais and other practices.
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3334480.3375056 (CHI 2020 Extended Abstracts (course))
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RI36LoVwk6w

### Bodystorming & Embodied Sketching

Ideating with and through moving bodies, props and play.

#### Sensory Bodystorming for Collocated Physical Training Design — Laia Turmo Vidal, Elena Márquez Segura (2018)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Vibration & haptics, Light & vision
- Idea: Sensory feedback is best designed inside the shared activity, where trainer and trainee feel each other's movement.
- What it is: A bodystorming variant in which trainers and trainees try out sensory props — lights, sounds, vibrations — during real training sessions to imagine technology that supports learning movement together.
- How it works: Workshops in circus and fitness training settings using simple sensory props, analysed for design qualities (with Annika Waern).
- Lens: Intercorporeality, Movement & expression
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3240167.3240224 (NordiCHI 2018)

#### Bodystorming for Movement-Based Interaction Design — Elena Márquez Segura, Laia Turmo Vidal (2016)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Posture, balance & proprioception
- Idea: For movement design, the ideas worth keeping are the ones that feel right in the body during the activity itself.
- What it is: Adapts bodystorming to movement-based interaction: participants explore movement, props and early technology together in the actual activity setting.
- How it works: Analysis of bodystorming sessions in movement-based design projects (with Asreen Rostami).
- Lens: Movement & expression, First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.17011/ht/urn.201611174655 (Human Technology 12(2), 2016)

#### Embodied Sketching — Elena Márquez Segura, Laia Turmo Vidal (2016)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Touch & pressure
- Idea: Bodies at play are sketching tools; the felt, social qualities of an activity can be explored before they are engineered.
- What it is: A family of ideation methods in which designers and users sketch with their moving bodies, props and play in real settings, before any technology exists.
- How it works: Synthesis of several design projects (games, physiotherapy, dance) using bodystorming, embodied play tests and minimal props.
- Lens: Pleasure & play, First-person & felt experience, Intercorporeality
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/2858036.2858486 (CHI 2016)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tI1GUPjrXjQ

#### Bodystorming as Embodied Designing — Dennis Schleicher (2010)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Whole body-mind
- Idea: Acting a scenario out with the whole body produces knowledge that talking and sketching on paper do not.
- What it is: An interactions article that presents bodystorming as embodied thinking: physically acting out a situation to generate and test design ideas.
- How it works: Practitioner account with guidance for running bodystorming sessions (with Peter Jones and Oksana Kachur).
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Body-mind unity
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/1865245.1865256 (ACM interactions 17(6), 2010)

#### Embodied Imagination: A Hybrid Method of Designing for Intimacy — Lone Koefoed Hansen, Susan Kozel (2007)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Touch & pressure, Movement & kinesthesia
- Idea: Imagination is embodied: moving with another person makes possible technologies for intimacy felt before they are built.
- What it is: A method that combines dance improvisation with design scenarios so that participants imagine future intimate technologies through their own bodies.
- How it works: Workshops mixing improvisation scores, props and discussion, developed around wearable and intimate technologies.
- Lens: Intercorporeality, First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1080/14626260701743200 (Digital Creativity 18(4), 2007)

#### Understanding Contexts by Being There: Case Studies in Bodystorming — Antti Oulasvirta (2003)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Whole body-mind
- Idea: Ideas come differently when the body is in the situation; being there is a way of thinking.
- What it is: Case studies in which designers generated ideas while physically in the real setting — a bus, a home, a kindergarten — rather than in a meeting room.
- How it works: Four bodystorming case studies comparing idea quality and process with conventional brainstorming.
- Lens: First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1007/s00779-003-0238-7 (Personal and Ubiquitous Computing 7(2), 2003)

#### Experience Prototyping — Marion Buchenau, Jane Fulton Suri (2000)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Touch & pressure
- Idea: The quickest way to understand a felt experience is to have it, in your own body, with rough props.
- What it is: Describes IDEO's practice of letting designers and clients live through a situation — wearing a pager, carrying a heart-patient's load — to understand how a design would feel.
- How it works: Case descriptions from IDEO projects using role-play, props and simulated situations at different design stages.
- Lens: First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/347642.347802 (DIS 2000)

### Soma-Based Material Exploration

Feeling materials, actuators and biosignals on the body: Soma Bits, somaesthetic toolkits, somadata.

#### Soma Express Kit — Michi Kanda (2023)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Touch & pressure, Vibration & haptics, Voice, sound & listening
- Idea: Blind and low-vision people's heightened somatic sensitivity is expertise: designing from it produces non-visual, shared experiences for everyone.
- What it is: A toolkit of wearable physiological sensors with haptic and sound modules that lets people with visual impairment express their somaesthetic experience of a place to sighted partners in real time.
- How it works: Wearable physiological sensing combined with multi-sensory haptic and audio output modules, used in collaborative sessions (with Kai Kunze, Keio KMD).
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Intercorporeality, Body politics
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3627050.3631571 (IoT 2023)

#### Sketching Soma Bits — Charles Windlin, Kristina Höök (2022)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Heat & temperature, Vibration & haptics, Shape-change & inflatables
- Idea: Designers need materials they can feel on their own body within minutes; that immediacy is what lets the soma lead the design.
- What it is: Describes the Soma Bits toolkit — small, soft modules that give heat, vibration and shape-change on the body, plus sensors — and how designers use it to sketch felt experiences directly on their skin.
- How it works: Modular hardware toolkit with actuators and sensors in fabric shapes, used and refined in workshops and courses (with Jarmo Laaksolahti).
- Lens: Technology that touches back, First-person & felt experience, Attunement & appreciation
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3532106.3533510 (DIS 2022)

#### From Biodata to Somadata — Miquel Alfaras, Vasiliki Tsaknaki, Kristina Höök (2020)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Muscle tension (EMG), Skin conductance & arousal, Breath
- Idea: Data about the body becomes meaningful when it is felt by the body; turning biodata into somadata keeps experience and measurement together.
- What it is: Proposes 'somadata': biosignals such as muscle tension or breathing explored as design material through felt feedback on the body, instead of charts on a screen.
- How it works: Design workshops where designers wore EMG and breathing sensors mapped to sound, vibration and heat, analysed as material exploration.
- Lens: Body data & ambiguity, First-person & felt experience, Technology that touches back
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3313831.3376684 (CHI 2020)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zohoISiOZmQ

#### Crafting the Body-Tool — Claudia Núñez-Pacheco, Lian Loke (2014)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Touch & pressure, Whole body-mind
- Idea: A wearable is not added to the body; it becomes part of how the body feels itself.
- What it is: A body-centred perspective on wearables in which the body and the worn object are crafted together as one 'body-tool' through felt exploration.
- How it works: Design study with wearable props and somatic exploration, drawing on phenomenology and somaesthetics.
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Attunement & appreciation
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/2598510.2598546 (DIS 2014)

#### TECHTILE Toolkit — Kouta Minamizawa (2012)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Touch & pressure, Vibration & haptics, Voice, sound & listening
- Idea: Touch becomes a design material once it can be recorded and replayed: designers learn by feeling and remixing textures with their own hands.
- What it is: A kit of a microphone, an amplifier and vibration actuators that lets anyone record the feel of an action, such as pouring marbles into a cup, and play it back into another object or hand; used in workshops where people sketch with touch.
- How it works: Audio-based haptic recording: contact microphones capture vibration, which is amplified and sent to voice-coil actuators attached to cups, paper or skin.
- Lens: Technology that touches back, First-person & felt experience, Attunement & appreciation
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/2343456.2343478 (SIGGRAPH 2012 Emerging Technologies)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eoztAbSlpfU

### Articulating & Evaluating Felt Experience

Micro-phenomenology, body maps, soma trajectories, the Sensual Evaluation Instrument, somatic connoisseurship.

#### Body Maps: A Generative Tool for Soma-based Design — Karen Anne Cochrane, Kristina Mah, Anna Ståhl, Lian Loke (2022)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Touch & pressure, Pain & inner sensations
- Idea: Drawing where and how something is felt helps people notice and share sensations they could not put into words.
- What it is: Body maps — outlines of a body that people draw and colour to locate sensations and feelings — used as a generative tool for soma design rather than only as a record.
- How it works: Workshops with body-map templates, drawing materials and somatic exercises, analysed across several soma design studies.
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Attunement & appreciation
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3490149.3502262 (TEI 2022)

#### Focusing for Interaction Design: An Introspective Somatic Method — Claudia Núñez-Pacheco, Lian Loke (2022)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Pain & inner sensations
- Idea: Meaning often begins as an unclear bodily feeling; staying with it patiently lets understanding and emotion emerge together.
- What it is: Adapts Eugene Gendlin's Focusing into steps designers can use to attend to a vague bodily 'felt sense' and let it unfold into words, images and design ideas.
- How it works: Method developed through first-person studies and workshops (following the authors' 2018 Design Journal technique paper).
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Attunement & appreciation, Body-mind unity
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3491102.3501978 (CHI 2022)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=g5e0WW1k5Fc

#### Articulating Soma Experiences using Trajectories — Paul Tennent, Kristina Höök, Steve Benford, Vasiliki Tsaknaki (2021)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Breath, Movement & kinesthesia
- Idea: A soma experience has a shape in time; mapping that shape helps designers orchestrate transitions rather than single effects.
- What it is: Soma trajectories: a way of drawing and describing how a felt experience unfolds over time — moments of attention, sensation and transition — for design and analysis.
- How it works: Extends Benford's trajectories framework with first-person accounts from several soma design projects.
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Body-mind unity
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3411764.3445482 (CHI 2021)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7ACi7LS9mj4

#### Articulating Experience: Reflections from Experts Applying Micro-Phenomenology to Design Research in HCI — Mirjana Prpa, Sarah Fdili Alaoui, Thecla Schiphorst (2020)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Breath
- Idea: Getting people to describe how an experience unfolds — not what they think about it — takes practice from both interviewer and interviewee.
- What it is: Interviews expert practitioners on how they use micro-phenomenology in HCI design research, and draws out its strengths, pitfalls and training needs.
- How it works: Qualitative study with experienced micro-phenomenology practitioners in design and HCI (with Philippe Pasquier).
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Attunement & appreciation
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3313831.3376664 (CHI 2020)

#### Designing for Kinesthetic Awareness — Jules Françoise, Yves Candau, Sarah Fdili Alaoui, Thecla Schiphorst (2017)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Voice, sound & listening
- Idea: Subtle changes in bodily awareness are invisible to logs and surveys; guided second-person inquiry can bring them into words.
- What it is: Studies an interactive movement-sonification system and uses second-person interviews, inspired by micro-phenomenology, to reveal how people's kinesthetic awareness shifts.
- How it works: User study with a sonification system and second-person interview protocol, analysed for dimensions of kinesthetic experience.
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3025453.3025714 (CHI 2017)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6pqkj8FbWaA

#### Tacit Narratives: Surfacing Aesthetic Meaning by Using Wearable Props and Focusing — Claudia Núñez-Pacheco, Lian Loke (2017)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Touch & pressure, Whole body-mind
- Idea: Objects on the body can act as handles for felt meanings that are otherwise hard to reach.
- What it is: Participants wear simple props and follow Focusing steps to bring tacit, bodily meanings of an experience into narrative.
- How it works: Workshop study combining wearable props, guided Focusing and narrative analysis.
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Attunement & appreciation
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3024969.3024979 (TEI 2017)

#### Self-Evidence: Applying Somatic Connoisseurship to Experience Design — Thecla Schiphorst (2011)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Breath, Touch & pressure
- Idea: The felt sense, when trained, is evidence; designers can learn to trust and articulate it.
- What it is: Introduces somatic connoisseurship: using the trained felt sense of somatic practitioners as evidence in designing and evaluating experience.
- How it works: Essay drawing on somatics (Laban, Feldenkrais, Body-Mind Centering) and the author's wearable and installation works.
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Attunement & appreciation
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/1979742.1979640 (CHI 2011 Extended Abstracts)

#### Design Representations of Moving Bodies for Interactive, Motion-Sensing Spaces — Lian Loke, Toni Robertson (2009)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Posture, balance & proprioception
- Idea: How a designer notates movement shapes what they can feel and design; choose representations that keep the mover's experience.
- What it is: Compares ways of representing movement for design — Labanotation, video, scores, personas of movement — and what each lets designers see about the moving body.
- How it works: Design case of an interactive motion-sensing space, with representations drawn from dance notation and interaction design.
- Lens: Movement & expression
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ijhcs.2008.11.003 (International Journal of Human-Computer Studies 67(4), 2009)

#### On Being Supple: In Search of Rigor without Rigidity — Katherine Isbister, Kristina Höök (2009)
- Type: Paper · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Whole body-mind
- Idea: Evaluating felt, bodily experience needs methods as flexible as the experience itself.
- What it is: Proposes 'supple' interaction — social, emotional, bodily — and asks how to evaluate it with rigour but without the rigid metrics of task performance.
- How it works: Position paper drawing on the authors' affective and movement-based systems (eMoto, SenToy, games).
- Lens: Affective loops, First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/1518701.1519042 (CHI 2009)

#### The Micro-Phenomenological Interview — Claire Petitmengin (2006)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Whole body-mind
- Idea: Much of felt experience is pre-reflective; careful questioning can bring it into awareness without imposing interpretations.
- What it is: An interview technique that guides a person back into one specific past moment and helps them describe, in fine detail, what they felt and did — gestures, sensations, shifts of attention.
- How it works: Second-person interview protocol (evocation of a singular experience, open 'how' questions, redirection from content to process).
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Attunement & appreciation
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1007/s11097-006-9022-2 (Phenomenology and the Cognitive Sciences 5, 2006)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YFU9YVuNXQg
- Project page: https://www.microphenomenology.com

#### The Sensual Evaluation Instrument — Katherine Isbister, Kristina Höök (2006)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Touch & pressure, Whole body-mind
- Idea: Feelings are bodily; letting people answer with their hands captures affect that words and rating scales flatten.
- What it is: A set of eight smooth, abstract sculpted objects that people hold and arrange to show how they feel while using a system, as a nonverbal alternative to questionnaires.
- How it works: Hand-sized sculpted objects made by an artist co-author, tested in user and cross-cultural studies (with Jarmo Laaksolahti).
- Lens: Affective loops, First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/1124772.1124946 (CHI 2006)

### Strong Concepts, Qualities & Ethics

Intermediate-level design knowledge from soma design: strong concepts, design qualities, felt ethics.

#### Change and Be Changed: Looking Back at 10 Years of the Soma Design Research Program — Kristina Höök, Jonas Löwgren, Anna Ståhl (2026)
- Type: Paper · Body & senses: Whole body-mind
- Idea: Soma design changes the designer as much as the design; a research program built on the felt body must account for that change.
- What it is: A TOCHI retrospective on ten years of the soma design research program: what its methods, prototypes and concepts achieved, and how doing it changed the designers themselves.
- How it works: Retrospective analysis of design examples and concepts, assessing the program's generativity, transferability and scope.
- Lens: Body-mind unity, First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3793670 (ACM TOCHI 2026)

#### Five Political Provocations for Soma Design: A Relational Perspective on Emotion and Politics — Rachael Garrett, Claudia Núñez-Pacheco, Kristina Höök (2024)
- Type: Paper · Body & senses: Whole body-mind
- Idea: Felt experience is shaped between bodies and by society; designing for the soma therefore means designing within politics.
- What it is: Five provocations on the politics of soma design — ways of knowing, the self, felt ethics and right action, the pursuit of happiness, and justice and the emotional labour of transformation.
- How it works: Conceptual paper combining soma design, political theory and Sara Ahmed's The Cultural Politics of Emotion (with Pasko Kisić-Merino and Pedro Sanches).
- Lens: Body politics, Intercorporeality
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3686169.3686213 (Halfway to the Future 2024)

#### Voicing Discomfort, Communicating Breakdowns: Design Exploration of Felt Ethics in a Body-Based Workshop — Kristina Popova (2024)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Touch & pressure, Movement & kinesthesia
- Idea: Ethical breakdowns are first felt as discomfort; giving people ways to voice it keeps bodies safe and design honest.
- What it is: A soma-design workshop with interaction designers that explores ethics through the discomfort felt in interpersonal bodily interaction, and how participants make that discomfort shared.
- How it works: Body-based workshop grounded in soma design and the ethics of care, analysed from video recordings and post-workshop interviews (with Arife Dila Demir, Joo Young Park and Rob Comber).
- Lens: Body politics, Intercorporeality, First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3679318.3685375 (NordiCHI 2024)

#### Felt Ethics: Cultivating Ethical Sensibility in Design Practice — Rachael Garrett, Kristina Popova, Claudia Núñez-Pacheco, Kristina Höök (2023)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Whole body-mind
- Idea: Ethics is not only reasoning after the fact; discomfort and care felt in the body during design are ethical signals worth attending to.
- What it is: Argues that ethical judgment in design is also felt, and describes practices that cultivate designers' bodily ethical sensibility during design work.
- How it works: Analysis of soma design and HRI design processes, identifying practices of felt ethical attention.
- Lens: Body-mind unity, Body politics
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3544548.3580875 (CHI 2023)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iVN61ogAV5k

#### Intercorporeal Biofeedback for Movement Learning — Laia Turmo Vidal, Elena Márquez Segura (2023)
- Type: Paper · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Muscle tension (EMG), Posture, balance & proprioception
- Idea: Movement is learned between bodies; biofeedback works best when it supports that intercorporeal exchange instead of a lone user and a screen.
- What it is: A strong concept: biofeedback shared between trainer and trainee, so that both can see or feel one body's inner activity and learn the movement together.
- How it works: Strong concept articulated from several training-technology design cases, with its design qualities (with Annika Waern).
- Lens: Intercorporeality, Body data & ambiguity
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3582428 (ACM TOCHI 30(3), 2023)

#### An Annotated Soma Design Process of the Pelvic Chair — Anna Ståhl, Madeline Balaam (2022)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Intimate & hormonal body, Posture, balance & proprioception
- Idea: Soma design knowledge lives in the process; annotating that process shows how felt judgments steer the design.
- What it is: An annotated portfolio of the whole soma design process behind the Pelvic Chair, a chair that helps people feel their pelvic floor, making each design move and its felt reason visible.
- How it works: Annotated portfolio method applied to a first-person soma design process (with Marianela Ciolfi Felice and Irene Kaklopoulou).
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Body politics
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3532106.3533469 (DIS 2022)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HspE-Al7_Y0

#### Making New Worlds: Transformative Becomings with Soma Design — Anna Ståhl, Madeline Balaam, Kristina Höök (2022)
- Type: Paper · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Intimate & hormonal body
- Idea: Working through the body transforms who we are; soma design is also a practice of changing how bodies are allowed to live.
- What it is: Argues that soma design changes designers and users, not only products, and reads soma design projects as ways of becoming — and of imagining other worlds for bodies.
- How it works: Theoretical reading of soma design cases through feminist new materialism and transformation (with Rob Comber).
- Lens: Body politics, Body-mind unity
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3491102.3502018 (CHI 2022)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yvlBEyPYZXw

#### Vulnerability as an Ethical Stance in Soma Design Processes — Kristina Popova, Rachael Garrett, Claudia Núñez-Pacheco, Kristina Höök (2022)
- Type: Paper · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Intimate & hormonal body
- Idea: Designing from the body means being exposed; vulnerability handled with care produces both better knowledge and fairer processes.
- What it is: Examines moments in soma design where designers expose their bodies and feelings, and argues that holding vulnerability with care is itself an ethical stance.
- How it works: Reflective analysis of first-person soma design processes and workshops (with Airi Lampinen).
- Lens: Body politics, First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3491102.3501994 (CHI 2022)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3eTw27UrRlQ

#### Unpacking Non-Dualistic Design: The Soma Design Case — Kristina Höök, Steve Benford, Paul Tennent, Vasiliki Tsaknaki (2021)
- Type: Paper · Body & senses: Whole body-mind
- Idea: Feeling, thinking and moving are one process; methods that treat the designer's soma as data make that non-dualism practical.
- What it is: A TOCHI article that uses a series of soma design projects to show what design looks like when it refuses the split between mind and body, self and other, subject and object.
- How it works: Theoretical paper built from case projects by KTH and Nottingham teams, analysed as design strategies for non-dualistic design.
- Lens: Body-mind unity, First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3462448 (ACM TOCHI 28(6), 2021)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RycXfX3vL4Q

#### Soma Design and Politics of the Body — Kristina Höök, Sara Eriksson, Marie Louise Juul Søndergaard (2019)
- Type: Paper · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Intimate & hormonal body
- Idea: Feeling your own body more finely also makes visible the social norms written into it; soma design has to engage those politics.
- What it is: A short paper arguing that design tends to repeat dichotomies such as body-mind, male-female and emotion-thinking, and that closer somatic engagement with digital materials can help designers bypass them and counter privilege.
- How it works: Position paper reflecting on the KTH group's soma design projects through non-dualism and feminist qualities such as pluralism and participation (with Ciolfi Felice, Campo Woytuk, Kilic Afsar, Tsaknaki and Ståhl).
- Lens: Body politics, Body-mind unity
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3363384.3363385 (Halfway to the Future Symposium 2019)

#### Designing with the Body: Somaesthetic Interaction Design — Kristina Höök (2018)
- Type: Book & essay · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Movement & kinesthesia
- Idea: Design starts from a body-mind trained to feel more finely; the designer's own soma is the first instrument of design, not an afterthought.
- What it is: Höök's book that defines soma design: it tells how she learned Feldenkrais and horseback riding, walks through the Soma Mat, Breathing Light and other cases, and sets out a soma design manifesto, theory and methods.
- How it works: Book (MIT Press, Design Thinking, Design Theory series) combining Shusterman's somaesthetics, first-person design accounts and a catalogue of methods such as estrangement, somatic training and soma-based material exploration.
- Lens: Body-mind unity, First-person & felt experience, Attunement & appreciation
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.7551/mitpress/11481.001.0001 (MIT Press 2018)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3oIngFrfZD4
- Images: https://covers.openlibrary.org/b/isbn/9780262038560-L.jpg?default=false
- Project page: https://mitpress.mit.edu/9780262038560/designing-with-the-body/

#### The Somatic Turn in Human-Computer Interaction — Lian Loke, Thecla Schiphorst (2018)
- Type: Paper · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Movement & kinesthesia
- Idea: Somatic practices give designers disciplined ways to attend to the body-mind as a whole, which is what makes soma design possible.
- What it is: An interactions article that names HCI's 'somatic turn': designers drawing on somatic practices to treat felt, first-person experience as central to design.
- How it works: Short overview essay linking Feldenkrais, Laban, Body-Mind Centering and other somatics to HCI methods.
- Lens: Body-mind unity, Attunement & appreciation
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3236675 (ACM interactions 25(5), 2018)

#### Soma-Based Design Theory — Kristina Höök, Caroline Hummels, Katherine Isbister, Elena Márquez Segura (2017)
- Type: Paper · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Movement & kinesthesia
- Idea: Theory of soma design is built by doing it together with bodies in the room, not only by reading.
- What it is: A CHI 2017 workshop where soma designers moved, touched and discussed together to work out a shared theory of soma-based design; its cases became the 2018 first-person paper.
- How it works: One-day workshop combining somatic exercises, sharing of first-person design cases and collective writing.
- Lens: Body-mind unity, First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3027063.3027082 (CHI 2017 Extended Abstracts)

#### Somaesthetic Appreciation Design — Kristina Höök, Anna Ståhl (2016)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Heat & temperature, Breath
- Idea: Systems for bodily awareness work when they guide lightly and leave room for the person's own felt sense to lead.
- What it is: The CHI paper behind the Soma Mat and Breathing Light that names design qualities for systems of somaesthetic appreciation: subtle guidance, making space and intimate correspondence.
- How it works: Research-through-design with Feldenkrais-trained designers, heat and light prototypes, and first-person and user studies.
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, Body-mind unity, Technology that touches back
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/2858036.2858583 (CHI 2016)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=uHTv_YqFjEk

#### Seven Principles to Design for Embodied Sensemaking — Caroline Hummels (2015)
- Type: Paper · Body & senses: Movement & kinesthesia, Whole body-mind
- Idea: People make sense of the world by moving in it; design should support that bodily sensemaking, not bypass it.
- What it is: Seven principles for designing systems that support sensemaking through the body, grounded in enactive cognition rather than information processing.
- How it works: Principles derived from enactivism and phenomenology and illustrated with TU/e design projects (with Jelle van Dijk).
- Lens: Body-mind unity, Movement & expression
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/2677199.2680577 (TEI 2015)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JVq80-6dLok

#### Somaesthetic Design — Kristina Höök, Anna Ståhl (2015)
- Type: Paper · Body & senses: Whole body-mind, Heat & temperature, Breath
- Idea: Technology can be designed to slow us down and help us feel our own body-mind more richly, instead of pulling attention outward.
- What it is: An interactions cover story that introduced somaesthetic design to a broad HCI audience, using the Soma Mat, heat and breath-driven light as examples of technology that turns attention inward.
- How it works: Magazine essay drawing on Feldenkrais-inspired design workshops and prototypes built in Höök's group.
- Lens: Body-mind unity, Attunement & appreciation
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/2770888 (ACM interactions 22(4), 2015)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=t22T4AwdU-E

#### Strong Concepts: Intermediate-Level Knowledge in Interaction Design Research — Kristina Höök, Jonas Löwgren (2012)
- Type: Paper · Body & senses: Whole body-mind
- Idea: Felt design knowledge can be shared without flattening it into rules: name the interaction pattern and show the cases.
- What it is: Defines strong concepts: design ideas that sit between a single artefact and a general theory, can be reused across designs, and are grounded in examples. Soma design uses them to share what it learns.
- How it works: Conceptual paper illustrated with strong concepts such as social navigation and seamfulness, later used for soma design concepts.
- Lens: Body-mind unity
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/2362364.2362371 (ACM TOCHI 19(3), 2012)

## Somaesthetic Interaction Design

Interactive systems that help us feel our own body-mind more richly: soma mats, breathing lights, heat, shape-change, sound and garments.

### Somaesthetic Appreciation & Attunement

Systems that slow us down and direct attention inward: Soma Mat, Breathing Light, guided body scans.

#### Soma Mat — Martin Jonsson, Anna Ståhl, Kristina Höök (2016)
- Type: Research prototype · Body & senses: Heat & temperature, Whole body-mind
- Idea: Slow, subtle heat does not signal anything; it quietly draws attention to parts of the body we rarely feel.
- What it is: The Soma Mat is a padded mat with heating elements beneath its surface; lying on it during a guided body scan, the listener feels warmth appear under the body part the recorded voice names.
- How it works: Heat pads under a sculpted felt surface are switched in sequence with a Feldenkrais-inspired body-scan recording; the user study examined heat as an aesthetic material (inertia, subtleness, heat transfer).
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, Technology that touches back, First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/2839462.2839487 (TEI 2016)
- Images: https://figures.semanticscholar.org/58851b397677d0295424829a85331041347eb3c2/3-Figure3-1.png https://figures.semanticscholar.org/1cde192fe629ab02c0c5bcfda880f59cce16e194/4-Figure2-1.png https://figures.semanticscholar.org/1cde192fe629ab02c0c5bcfda880f59cce16e194/3-Figure1-1.png

### Designing with Breath

Breath as input, output and shared rhythm.

#### Breathing Mirror — Ziyue Piao (2026)
- Type: Method & toolkit · Body & senses: Breath, Voice, sound & listening, Movement & kinesthesia
- Idea: Body data works best as a mirror for first-person recall, not as a verdict: seeing one's breath brings back the felt experience of playing.
- What it is: Pianists wear a breathing belt while playing and afterwards watch their breathing aligned with the music, using it to recall and describe what their body was doing at each moment.
- How it works: Stretch-sensor breathing belt synchronised with audio and MIDI, plus an annotation interface used in stimulated-recall interviews with pianists.
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Body data & ambiguity, Attunement & appreciation
- Paper: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01974 (NIME 2026)
- Images: https://arxiv.org/html/2609.01974v1/images/GummiStra_Belt.jpeg

#### Soma Corset — Pavel Karpashevich, Pedro Sanches, Rachael Garrett, Yoav Luft, Kelsey Cotton, Vasiliki Tsaknaki, Kristina Höök (2022)
- Type: Research prototype · Body & senses: Breath, Shape-change & inflatables, Touch & pressure
- Idea: When a garment mirrors your breath in tight loops, the line between wearer and technology blurs, and not always comfortably.
- What it is: The Soma Corset senses breathing around the torso and answers with inflating pads, so the garment can feel like a harmonious partner, an organic 'other' with its own will, or a twisted mirror of one's own breath.
- How it works: Breathing sensors and inflatable actuators placed over the diaphragm, intercostal muscles and lower back, run in closed sensing-actuation loops and explored by the design team over a long soma design process.
- Lens: Estrangement, Technology that touches back, More-than-human
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3490498 (ACM TOCHI 2022)
- Images: https://figures.semanticscholar.org/c493d77955a0fbf485736178fff1a2b4db941a38/9-Figure2-1.png https://figures.semanticscholar.org/c493d77955a0fbf485736178fff1a2b4db941a38/8-Figure1-1.png

#### Breathing Garment — Annkatrin Jung, Miquel Alfaras, Pavel Karpashevich, Kristina Höök (2021)
- Type: Research prototype · Body & senses: Breath, Touch & pressure, Shape-change & inflatables
- Idea: Deep pressure can direct attention to the muscles and bones of breathing; the most evocative rhythm sits between following and leading the breath.
- What it is: A garment with inflatable cushions presses firmly on the torso in relation to the wearer's breathing, sometimes following it, sometimes leading it.
- How it works: A breathing sensor drives pneumatic actuators placed symmetrically and asymmetrically on the torso, inspired by Deep Pressure Therapy; first-person explorations led to 'breathing correspondence' interactions.
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, Technology that touches back, Care & healing
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3411764.3445533 (CHI 2021)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=b_1t7TjxnCk

#### Breathing Shell — Vasiliki Tsaknaki, Kelsey Cotton, Pavel Karpashevich, Pedro Sanches (2021)
- Type: Research prototype · Body & senses: Breath, Shape-change & inflatables, Voice, sound & listening
- Idea: Being sensed is itself a bodily experience; a sensor that is felt makes the relation between body and data reciprocal.
- What it is: Designed with a classical singer, the Breathing Shell is a wearable breathing sensor that also presses back on the body, so the singer feels 'the sensor feeling you'.
- How it works: A new sensing mechanism built from shape-change (inflatable) technology around the singer's torso, developed through soma design sessions on non-habitual breathing.
- Lens: First-person & felt experience, Technology that touches back, Body data & ambiguity
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3411764.3445628 (CHI 2021)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bY82dGWywj8

#### aSpire — Kyung Yun Choi (2021)
- Type: Research prototype · Body & senses: Breath, Shape-change & inflatables, Touch & pressure
- Idea: Breath can be guided by touch rather than by instruction: a soft rhythm on the body invites slower breathing without adding mental load.
- What it is: A small pneumatic device clipped to a strap or belt inflates air pouches against the body at a slow, personalised rhythm, and breathing tends to follow; tested with car passengers during real commutes.
- How it works: Array of individually inflating air pouches driven by a mobile pump and valves, with adjustable pattern, intensity and frequency (MIT Media Lab).
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, Technology that touches back, Care & healing
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/3411763.3451602 (CHI 2021 Extended Abstracts)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2aIHXcMv-qM

#### Breathing Light — Anna Ståhl, Martin Jonsson, Kristina Höök (2016)
- Type: Research prototype · Body & senses: Breath, Light & vision
- Idea: Seeing your breath softly reflected in ambient light lets you notice breathing without trying to control it.
- What it is: Breathing Light is a lamp with a curtain of fabric and strings that encloses a person lying beneath it; the light slowly dims in and out with their breathing.
- How it works: A sensor on the chest measures breathing movement and drives a dimmable lamp inside a fabric and string enclosure; evaluated together with the Soma Mat, and later in a three-month home study.
- Lens: Attunement & appreciation, Body-mind unity, First-person & felt experience
- Paper: https://doi.org/10.1145/2851581.2889464 (CHI 2016 Extended Abstracts)
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4kAX5YRJkJw
- Images: https://figures.semanticscholar.org/58851b397677d0295424829a85331041347eb3c2/4-Figure4-1.png https://figures.semanticscholar.org/58851b397677d0295424829a85331041347eb3c2/2-Figure1-1.png

### Touching Back: Heat, Vibration & Shape-Change

Technology that acts on the skin and body: heat, vibration, inflatables, shape-changing surfaces.

#### Shape-Changing Car Seats — Madeline Balaam, Anna Ståhl, Guðrún Margrét Ívansdóttir, Kristina Höök, Caroline Yan Zheng (2024)
- Type: Research prototype · Body & senses: Shape-change & inflatables, Touch & pressure, Posture, balance & proprioception
- Idea: Touch on large, heavy parts of the body can link somatic experience to the rational tasks of driving, so the car's intelligence is felt rather than displayed.
- What it is: With a car manufacturer, the team co-designed touch patterns for a shape-changing driver's seat: re-engaging in driving, disengaging, saying farewell and being held while turning.
